People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Yeah… a little. I mean, I still go to work, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to function or anything.

I mean… I try not to, yeah. I don’t sit around talking about it unless I have to.

It’s more like I arrange things so I don’t have to deal with reminders of it. I’ve started trading shifts when I can, pushing back on late release windows, and if I’m there after dark I’ll use the side entrance instead of the lobby. Or I’ll work from home if there’s a reasonable excuse.

Sometimes, yeah. Not constantly or anything, but… certain things can just hit me out of nowhere.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m cutting everyone off or anything, but I’ve definitely been arranging things differently.

I’ve started trading shifts when I can, and pushing back on release windows if they’d keep me there late. If I do have to be there after dark, I’ll take the side entrance, or I’ll try to work from home if that’s reasonable.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.

At work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “permanently,” because that sounds so… final. And I know that’s probably not a helpful way to think about it.

But yes, I do have this feeling that something in me is different now. Like work doesn’t feel safe anymore, even when people tell me it is. And I keep thinking that the version of me who got promoted, who was competent and could handle things, isn’t really there in the same way.

Yeah… I do, sometimes. I try not to call it “failure,” because I know that’s a harsh word, but that’s kind of where my mind goes.

I keep thinking, like… I was promoted because I was competent, because people trusted me to handle things. And now I don’t feel like that person is fully there anymore. Work doesn’t feel safe to me the way it used to, and I start wondering what that says about me — like maybe I’m not as capable as everyone thought.

I don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning. But there’s definitely this feeling that I’m somehow damaged now, or not enough in a way I wasn’t before. Is that what you mean?

Maybe I’m quieter sometimes. But I’m not sure if that counts as not enjoying things. I don’t know if I’d go as far as saying I feel worthless, or that my whole life has no meaning. That feels… a little too strong, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the break-in at work, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not constantly or anything, but… certain things can just hit me out of nowhere.\n\nLike if a badge scanner beeps, or I hear heavy footsteps in the hallway, or one of those late-night deployment alerts comes through, it’s like my brain suddenly puts me back in that office. My chest gets tight, and I’ll get this image of the guard on the floor that just cuts through whatever I’m doing.\n\nI know it’s not literally happening again, but for a moment it feels… very close to that. Is that what you mean by reliving it?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid people or activities that remind you of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m cutting everyone off or anything, but I’ve definitely been arranging things differently.\n\nI’ve started trading shifts when I can, and pushing back on release windows if they’d keep me there late. If I do have to be there after dark, I’ll take the side entrance, or I’ll try to work from home if that’s reasonable.\n\nIt’s mostly that lobby at night. Walking through it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so I try not to put myself in that position. Is that what you mean by activities that remind me?\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling of not being safe at work or your reactions to reminders of the break-in affected your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. I’ve missed some migration deadlines, and I’ve avoided being the person on key late deployments when I can. That’s caused some tension with colleagues, and I got called out in a performance review for conflicts that… honestly, I don’t think would’ve happened before.\n\nAnd socially, or professionally I guess, I’ve pulled back from mentoring and some women-in-tech leadership events. I used to be pretty involved, but lately I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to show up the way people expect me to.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of relationship impact you mean, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change… not in a dramatic way, at least.\n\nI still talk to people, and I still try to show up when I’m supposed to. I guess I’ve been a little less enthusiastic about making plans, but I don’t know if that’s because of the break-in specifically or just being tired and trying to keep things manageable.\n\nWith family, I don’t think they’d say I’ve disappeared or anything. Maybe I’m quieter sometimes. But I’m not sure if that counts as not enjoying things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently changed for the worse because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “permanently,” because that sounds so… final. And I know that’s probably not a helpful way to think about it.\n\nBut yes, I do have this feeling that something in me is different now. Like work doesn’t feel safe anymore, even when people tell me it is. I don’t want to be dramatic. But there’s this thought that I’m damaged now, or less capable somehow, and I can’t seem to shake it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a failure or inadequate because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, sometimes. I know that probably sounds vague, sorry.\n\nIt’s like I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t really safe anymore, even when I’m trying to tell myself it should be fine. And… I don’t know, I feel kind of damaged now. Like the person who was competent enough to get promoted—that version of me doesn’t feel like she’s really there anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling that work isn't safe anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s probably not that dramatic, I know people have stressful jobs. But for me, it’s like even when nothing bad is happening, I don’t really believe work is a safe place anymore.\n\nI keep thinking I should be able to handle it because I was promoted, and I used to feel competent. But now I feel like that version of me is kind of… gone, or broken somehow. Like maybe I’m not the person everyone thought I was. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen at work that made you start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was. I probably should’ve said that more clearly.\n\nIt started after a break-in at work, during an overnight deployment. That was… about two years and four months ago now, I think. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it was, but that’s when things started feeling different for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places or situations since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I still go to work, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to function or anything.\n\nBut I’ve started… trading shifts when I can, or pushing back on release windows if they’re going to run late. And if I do have to be there, I’ll take the side entrance instead of walking through the lobby after dark. Sometimes I’ll ask to work from home if there’s a way to justify it.\n\nIt’s just that walking through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud. But that’s what it feels like. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try not to, yeah. I don’t sit around talking about it unless I have to.\n\nIt’s more like I arrange things so I don’t have to deal with reminders of it. I’ve started trading shifts when I can, pushing back on late release windows, and if I’m there after dark I’ll use the side entrance instead of the lobby. Or I’ll work from home if there’s a reasonable excuse.\n\nI know that’s not exactly “talking or thinking,” but walking through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so… I guess I do avoid it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the break-in at work, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not constantly or anything, but… certain things can just hit me out of nowhere.\n\nLike if a badge scanner beeps, or I hear heavy footsteps in the hallway, or one of those late-night deployment alerts comes through, it’s like my brain suddenly puts me back in that office. My chest gets tight, and I’ll get this image of the guard on the floor that just cuts through whatever I’m doing.\n\nI know it’s not literally happening again, but for a moment it feels… very close to that. Is that what you mean by reliving it?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid people or activities that remind you of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m cutting everyone off or anything, but I’ve definitely been arranging things differently.\n\nI’ve started trading shifts when I can, and pushing back on release windows if they’d keep me there late. If I do have to be there after dark, I’ll take the side entrance, or I’ll try to work from home if that’s reasonable.\n\nIt’s mostly that lobby at night. Walking through it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so I try not to put myself in that position. Is that what you mean by activities that remind me?\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling of not being safe at work or your reactions to reminders of the break-in affected your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. I’ve missed some migration deadlines, and I’ve avoided being the person on key late deployments when I can. That’s caused some tension with colleagues, and I got called out in a performance review for conflicts that… honestly, I don’t think would’ve happened before.\n\nAnd socially, or professionally I guess, I’ve pulled back from mentoring and some women-in-tech leadership events. I used to be pretty involved, but lately I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to show up the way people expect me to.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of relationship impact you mean, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change… not in a dramatic way, at least.\n\nI still talk to people, and I still try to show up when I’m supposed to. I guess I’ve been a little less enthusiastic about making plans, but I don’t know if that’s because of the break-in specifically or just being tired and trying to keep things manageable.\n\nWith family, I don’t think they’d say I’ve disappeared or anything. Maybe I’m quieter sometimes. But I’m not sure if that counts as not enjoying things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently changed for the worse because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “permanently,” because that sounds so… final. And I know that’s probably not a helpful way to think about it.\n\nBut yes, I do have this feeling that something in me is different now. Like work doesn’t feel safe anymore, even when people tell me it is. And I keep thinking that the version of me who got promoted, who was competent and could handle things, isn’t really there in the same way.\n\nI’m not saying I’m ruined or anything. I don’t want to be dramatic. But there’s this thought that I’m damaged now, or less capable somehow, and I can’t seem to shake it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a failure or inadequate because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, sometimes. I try not to call it “failure,” because I know that’s a harsh word, but that’s kind of where my mind goes.\n\nI keep thinking, like… I was promoted because I was competent, because people trusted me to handle things. And now I don’t feel like that person is fully there anymore. Work doesn’t feel safe to me the way it used to, and I start wondering what that says about me — like maybe I’m not as capable as everyone thought.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning. But there’s definitely this feeling that I’m somehow damaged now, or not enough in a way I wasn’t before. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're worthless or that your life has no meaning because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d go as far as saying I feel worthless, or that my whole life has no meaning. That feels… a little too strong, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut I am here because I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, and that something about me is damaged now. Like the person who got promoted — the competent version of me — isn’t really there in the same way anymore.\n\nSo… not meaningless, exactly. More like I keep questioning myself in a way I didn’t before. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to trust yourself to make good decisions because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least somewhat. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t trust myself at all, but I do second-guess myself more now.\n\nI keep thinking, if work felt safe before and then it didn’t, maybe my judgment about things isn’t as solid as I thought. And then I get this feeling that I’m not the same competent person who got promoted, like something in me got damaged somehow.\n\nI know that may sound a little dramatic. I’m still making decisions and getting through things. But yes, I do question whether I can trust myself the way I used to. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s part of it.

I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle anything anymore, because I am still doing things and trying to keep up. But since what happened at work, I do feel less confident taking on responsibility. Like work doesn’t feel safe in the same way, and I keep wondering if I’m somehow… damaged now.

It’s like the version of me who got promoted, who could handle pressure and be trusted with things, doesn’t feel fully there anymore. I know that may sound a little harsh, but that’s honestly where my mind goes sometimes.

My chest gets tight, and I’ll get this image of the guard on the floor that just cuts through whatever I’m doing.\n\nI know it’s not literally happening again, but for a moment it feels… very close to that. Is that what you mean by activities that remind me?\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling of not being safe at work or your reactions to reminders of the break-in affected your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… yes, I do, although I know maybe that isn’t completely fair.

I keep going over it and thinking that if I had pushed harder for better security during the overnight deployment, or if I had called someone sooner, maybe the guard wouldn’t have been hurt as badly. And then another part of me knows I was hiding and terrified, so it’s not like I was calmly choosing what to do.

But yes… I do blame myself in that way. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because things haven’t felt right for a while. Especially with work. I know that probably sounds vague, sorry.\n\nIt’s like I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t really safe anymore, even when I’m trying to tell myself it should be fine. And… I don’t know, I feel kind of damaged now. Like the person who was competent enough to get promoted—that version of me doesn’t feel like she’s really there anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling that work isn't safe anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s probably not that dramatic, I know people have stressful jobs. But for me, it’s like even when nothing bad is happening, I don’t really believe work is a safe place anymore.\n\nI keep thinking I should be able to handle it because I was promoted, and I used to feel competent. But now I feel like that version of me is kind of… gone, or broken somehow. Like maybe I’m not the person everyone thought I was. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen at work that made you start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was. I probably should’ve said that more clearly.\n\nIt started after a break-in at work, during an overnight deployment. That was… about two years and four months ago now, I think. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it was, but that’s when things started feeling different for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places or situations since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I still go to work, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to function or anything.\n\nBut I’ve started… trading shifts when I can, or pushing back on release windows if they’re going to run late. And if I do have to be there, I’ll take the side entrance instead of walking through the lobby after dark. Sometimes I’ll ask to work from home if there’s a way to justify it.\n\nIt’s just that walking through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud. But that’s what it feels like. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try not to, yeah. I don’t sit around talking about it unless I have to.\n\nIt’s more like I arrange things so I don’t have to deal with reminders of it. I’ve started trading shifts when I can, pushing back on late release windows, and if I’m there after dark I’ll use the side entrance instead of the lobby. Or I’ll work from home if there’s a reasonable excuse.\n\nI know that’s not exactly “talking or thinking,” but walking through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so… I guess I do avoid it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the break-in at work, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not constantly or anything, but… certain things can just hit me out of nowhere.\n\nLike if a badge scanner beeps, or I hear heavy footsteps in the hallway, or one of those late-night deployment alerts comes through, it’s like my brain suddenly puts me back in that office. My chest gets tight, and I’ll get this image of the guard on the floor that just cuts through whatever I’m doing.\n\nI know it’s not literally happening again, but for a moment it feels… very close to that. Is that what you mean by reliving it?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid people or activities that remind you of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m cutting everyone off or anything, but I’ve definitely been arranging things differently.\n\nI’ve started trading shifts when I can, and pushing back on release windows if they’d keep me there late. If I do have to be there after dark, I’ll take the side entrance, or I’ll try to work from home if that’s reasonable.\n\nIt’s mostly that lobby at night. Walking through it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so I try not to put myself in that position. Is that what you mean by activities that remind me?\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling of not being safe at work or your reactions to reminders of the break-in affected your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. I’ve missed some migration deadlines, and I’ve avoided being the person on key late deployments when I can. That’s caused some tension with colleagues, and I got called out in a performance review for conflicts that… honestly, I don’t think would’ve happened before.\n\nAnd socially, or professionally I guess, I’ve pulled back from mentoring and some women-in-tech leadership events. I used to be pretty involved, but lately I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to show up the way people expect me to.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of relationship impact you mean, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change… not in a dramatic way, at least.\n\nI still talk to people, and I still try to show up when I’m supposed to. I guess I’ve been a little less enthusiastic about making plans, but I don’t know if that’s because of the break-in specifically or just being tired and trying to keep things manageable.\n\nWith family, I don’t think they’d say I’ve disappeared or anything. Maybe I’m quieter sometimes. But I’m not sure if that counts as not enjoying things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently changed for the worse because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “permanently,” because that sounds so… final. Like work doesn’t feel safe anymore, even when people tell me it is. I don’t want to be dramatic. But there’s this thought that I’m damaged now, or less capable somehow, and I can’t seem to shake it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a failure or inadequate because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, sometimes. I try not to call it “failure,” because I know that’s a harsh word, but that’s kind of where my mind goes.\n\nI keep thinking, like… I was promoted because I was competent, because people trusted me to handle things. I’ve started trading shifts when I can, pushing back on late release windows, and if I’m there after dark I’ll use the side entrance instead of the lobby. Or I’ll work from home if there’s a reasonable excuse.\n\nI know that’s not exactly “talking or thinking,” but walking through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so… I guess I do avoid it. Is that what you mean by reliving it?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid people or activities that remind you of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m cutting everyone off or anything, but I’ve definitely been arranging things differently.\n\nI’ve started trading shifts when I can, and pushing back on release windows if they’d keep me there late. If I do have to be there after dark, I’ll take the side entrance, or I’ll try to work from home if that’s reasonable.\n\nIt’s mostly that lobby at night. Walking through it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so I try not to put myself in that position. Is that what you mean by activities that remind me?\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling of not being safe at work or your reactions to reminders of the break-in affected your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. I’ve missed some migration deadlines, and I’ve avoided being the person on key late deployments when I can. That’s caused some tension with colleagues, and I got called out in a performance review for conflicts that… honestly, I don’t think would’ve happened before.\n\nAnd socially, or professionally I guess, I’ve pulled back from mentoring and some women-in-tech leadership events. I used to be pretty involved, but lately I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to show up the way people expect me to.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of relationship impact you mean, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change… not in a dramatic way, at least.\n\nI still talk to people, and I still try to show up when I’m supposed to. I guess I’ve been a little less enthusiastic about making plans, but I don’t know if that’s because of the break-in specifically or just being tired and trying to keep things manageable.\n\nWith family, I don’t think they’d say I’ve disappeared or anything. Maybe I’m quieter sometimes. But I’m not sure if that counts as not enjoying things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently changed for the worse because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “permanently,” because that sounds so… final. Like work doesn’t feel safe anymore, even when people tell me it is. I don’t want to be dramatic. But there’s this thought that I’m damaged now, or less capable somehow, and I can’t seem to shake it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a failure or inadequate because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, sometimes. I try not to call it “failure,” because I know that’s a harsh word, but that’s kind of where my mind goes.\n\nI keep thinking, like… I was promoted because I was competent, because people trusted me to handle things. And now I don’t feel like that person is fully there anymore. Work doesn’t feel safe to me the way it used to, and I start wondering what that says about me — like maybe I’m not as capable as everyone thought.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning. But there’s definitely this feeling that I’m somehow damaged now, or not enough in a way I wasn’t before. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're worthless or that your life has no meaning because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d go as far as saying I feel worthless, or that my whole life has no meaning. That feels… a little too strong, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut I am here because I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, and that something about me is damaged now. Is that what you mean by reliving it?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid people or activities that remind you of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m cutting everyone off or anything, but I’ve definitely been arranging things differently.\n\nI’ve started trading shifts when I can, and pushing back on release windows if they’d keep me there late. If I do have to be there after dark, I’ll take the side entrance, or I’ll try to work from home if that’s reasonable.\n\nIt’s mostly that lobby at night. Walking through it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so I try not to put myself in that position. Is that what you mean by activities that remind me?\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling of not being safe at work or your reactions to reminders of the break-in affected your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. I’ve missed some migration deadlines, and I’ve avoided being the person on key late deployments when I can. That’s caused some tension with colleagues, and I got called out in a performance review for conflicts that… honestly, I don’t think would’ve happened before.\n\nAnd socially, or professionally I guess, I’ve pulled back from mentoring and some women-in-tech leadership events. I used to be pretty involved, but lately I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to show up the way people expect me to.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of relationship impact you mean, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change… not in a dramatic way, at least.\n\nI still talk to people, and I still try to show up when I’m supposed to. I guess I’ve been a little less enthusiastic about making plans, but I don’t know if that’s because of the break-in specifically or just being tired and trying to keep things manageable.\n\nWith family, I don’t think they’d say I’ve disappeared or anything. Maybe I’m quieter sometimes. But I’m not sure if that counts as not enjoying things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently changed for the worse because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “permanently,” because that sounds so… final. And I know that’s probably not a helpful way to think about it.\n\nBut yes, I do have this feeling that something in me is different now. Like work doesn’t feel safe anymore, even when people tell me it is. And I keep thinking that the version of me who got promoted, who was competent and could handle things, isn’t really there in the same way.\n\nI’m not saying I’m ruined or anything. I don’t want to be dramatic. But there’s this thought that I’m damaged now, or less capable somehow, and I can’t seem to shake it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a failure or inadequate because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, sometimes. I try not to call it “failure,” because I know that’s a harsh word, but that’s kind of where my mind goes.\n\nI keep thinking, like… I was promoted because I was competent, because people trusted me to handle things. And now I don’t feel like that person is fully there anymore. Work doesn’t feel safe to me the way it used to, and I start wondering what that says about me — like maybe I’m not as capable as everyone thought.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning. But there’s definitely this feeling that I’m somehow damaged now, or not enough in a way I wasn’t before. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're worthless or that your life has no meaning because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d go as far as saying I feel worthless, or that my whole life has no meaning. That feels… a little too strong, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut I am here because I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, and that something about me is damaged now. I’m still making decisions and getting through things. But yes, I do question whether I can trust myself the way I used to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're less capable of handling challenges or responsibilities because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle anything anymore, because I am still doing things and trying to keep up. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because things haven’t felt right for a while. Especially with work. I know that probably sounds vague, sorry.\n\nIt’s like I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t really safe anymore, even when I’m trying to tell myself it should be fine. And… I don’t know, I feel kind of damaged now. Like the person who was competent enough to get promoted—that version of me doesn’t feel like she’s really there anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling that work isn't safe anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s probably not that dramatic, I know people have stressful jobs. But for me, it’s like even when nothing bad is happening, I don’t really believe work is a safe place anymore.\n\nI keep thinking I should be able to handle it because I was promoted, and I used to feel competent. But now I feel like that version of me is kind of… gone, or broken somehow. Like maybe I’m not the person everyone thought I was. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen at work that made you start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was. I probably should’ve said that more clearly.\n\nIt started after a break-in at work, during an overnight deployment. That was… about two years and four months ago now, I think. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it was, but that’s when things started feeling different for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places or situations since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I still go to work, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to function or anything.\n\nBut I’ve started… trading shifts when I can, or pushing back on release windows if they’re going to run late. And if I do have to be there, I’ll take the side entrance instead of walking through the lobby after dark. Sometimes I’ll ask to work from home if there’s a way to justify it.\n\nIt’s just that walking through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud. But that’s what it feels like. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try not to, yeah. I don’t sit around talking about it unless I have to.\n\nIt’s more like I arrange things so I don’t have to deal with reminders of it. I’ve started trading shifts when I can, pushing back on late release windows, and if I’m there after dark I’ll use the side entrance instead of the lobby. Or I’ll work from home if there’s a reasonable excuse.\n\nI know that’s not exactly “talking or thinking,” but walking through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so… I guess I do avoid it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the break-in at work, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not constantly or anything, but… certain things can just hit me out of nowhere.\n\nLike if a badge scanner beeps, or I hear heavy footsteps in the hallway, or one of those late-night deployment alerts comes through, it’s like my brain suddenly puts me back in that office. My chest gets tight, and I’ll get this image of the guard on the floor that just cuts through whatever I’m doing.\n\nI know it’s not literally happening again, but for a moment it feels… very close to that. Is that what you mean by reliving it?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid people or activities that remind you of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m cutting everyone off or anything, but I’ve definitely been arranging things differently.\n\nI’ve started trading shifts when I can, and pushing back on release windows if they’d keep me there late. If I do have to be there after dark, I’ll take the side entrance, or I’ll try to work from home if that’s reasonable.\n\nIt’s mostly that lobby at night. Walking through it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart, so I try not to put myself in that position. Is that what you mean by activities that remind me?\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling of not being safe at work or your reactions to reminders of the break-in affected your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work I’ve been trying to keep it from becoming everyone else’s problem, but my performance has definitely slipped. I’ve missed some migration deadlines, and I’ve avoided being the person on key late deployments when I can. That’s caused some tension with colleagues, and I got called out in a performance review for conflicts that… honestly, I don’t think would’ve happened before.\n\nAnd socially, or professionally I guess, I’ve pulled back from mentoring and some women-in-tech leadership events. I used to be pretty involved, but lately I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to show up the way people expect me to.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of relationship impact you mean, but… yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to enjoy activities or spend time with family and friends since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change… not in a dramatic way, at least.\n\nI still talk to people, and I still try to show up when I’m supposed to. I guess I’ve been a little less enthusiastic about making plans, but I don’t know if that’s because of the break-in specifically or just being tired and trying to keep things manageable.\n\nWith family, I don’t think they’d say I’ve disappeared or anything. Maybe I’m quieter sometimes. But I’m not sure if that counts as not enjoying things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently changed for the worse because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “permanently,” because that sounds so… final. And I know that’s probably not a helpful way to think about it.\n\nBut yes, I do have this feeling that something in me is different now. Like work doesn’t feel safe anymore, even when people tell me it is. And I keep thinking that the version of me who got promoted, who was competent and could handle things, isn’t really there in the same way.\n\nI’m not saying I’m ruined or anything. I don’t want to be dramatic. But there’s this thought that I’m damaged now, or less capable somehow, and I can’t seem to shake it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a failure or inadequate because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, sometimes. I try not to call it “failure,” because I know that’s a harsh word, but that’s kind of where my mind goes.\n\nI keep thinking, like… I was promoted because I was competent, because people trusted me to handle things. And now I don’t feel like that person is fully there anymore. Work doesn’t feel safe to me the way it used to, and I start wondering what that says about me — like maybe I’m not as capable as everyone thought.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. I’m still functioning. But there’s definitely this feeling that I’m somehow damaged now, or not enough in a way I wasn’t before. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're worthless or that your life has no meaning because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d go as far as saying I feel worthless, or that my whole life has no meaning. That feels… a little too strong, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut I am here because I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, and that something about me is damaged now. Like the person who got promoted — the competent version of me — isn’t really there in the same way anymore.\n\nSo… not meaningless, exactly. More like I keep questioning myself in a way I didn’t before. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to trust yourself to make good decisions because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least somewhat. I don’t know if it’s that I can’t trust myself at all, but I do second-guess myself more now.\n\nI keep thinking, if work felt safe before and then it didn’t, maybe my judgment about things isn’t as solid as I thought. And then I get this feeling that I’m not the same competent person who got promoted, like something in me got damaged somehow.\n\nI know that may sound a little dramatic. I’m still making decisions and getting through things. But yes, I do question whether I can trust myself the way I used to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're less capable of handling challenges or responsibilities because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle anything anymore, because I am still doing things and trying to keep up. But since what happened at work, I do feel less confident taking on responsibility. Like work doesn’t feel safe in the same way, and I keep wondering if I’m somehow… damaged now.\n\nIt’s like the version of me who got promoted, who could handle pressure and be trusted with things, doesn’t feel fully there anymore. I know that may sound a little harsh, but that’s honestly where my mind goes sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you are to blame for what happened at work?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I do, although I know maybe that isn’t completely fair.\n\nI keep going over it and thinking that if I had pushed harder for better security during the overnight deployment, or if I had called someone sooner, maybe the guard wouldn’t have been hurt as badly. And then another part of me knows I was hiding and terrified, so it’s not like I was calmly choosing what to do.\n\nBut yes… I do blame myself in that way. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you are fundamentally flawed or defective as a person because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sometimes. I don’t really like saying “fundamentally flawed,” because that sounds so extreme, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I do have this feeling that something in me is damaged now. Like work used to feel safe, and I used to feel like I was capable — I got promoted, people trusted me — and now I don’t feel like that person is really there in the same way.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I do think that at times. Not all the time, but enough that it bothers me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're no longer the person you used to be before the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely different or that I’ve lost everything about myself, because I know that’s probably not true. But it does feel like the person I was before the break-in — the person who felt competent at work, who got promoted and could handle responsibility — isn’t really there in the same way now.\n\nI keep feeling like work isn’t safe anymore, and then I start thinking maybe something in me is damaged now. I know that may sound dramatic, but that’s honestly how it feels sometimes. Is that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you are less competent or less capable as a person because of what happened at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think that sometimes.\n\nI don’t mean I think I’m incapable at everything, or that I was never good at my job.
